Some stepping-stone alternatives which could contribute to putting a team in a great position to achieve the biggest wins (but which we expect are unlikely to win the biggest prizes without follow-up work):</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>A comparative analysis</span></b><span> repeatably</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref95g2ovdewj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn95g2ovdewj">[5]</a></sup></span><span> applying two or more different AI assessment methodologies to the same (sub-)questions from the topic, with explicit discussion of where they agree and diverge. What downstream considerations do they best enable? What are their strengths and shortcomings? What kinds of supporting epistemic metadata would help them to work better?</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>A critique with counterexamples</span></b><span> of an otherwise promising approach, demonstrating the importance of further work or indicating less tractability than we might have thought.</span></li></ul><p><span>Optionally, submit a description of your plan or a briefer, less complete implementation of it by Jun 21, 2026, and we will weigh in on whether the work seems on track for a prize (and potentially provide feedback). Use </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLScHGLJRH5ex27i0hpL0wPqyZFqp1ykYqFmJxgvg_zYKD6g1mw/viewform"><span>the main submission form</span></a><span> and check the </span><i><span>early feedback</span></i><span> box.</span></p><p><span>What we care about most: </span><b><span>Would this actually help someone reason better about this case? Does it generalize? Does it scale with improvements to AI or more compute? Does it compound, with multiple people or teams building on each others’ work?</span></b></p><p><span>We’ll ask judges to use the following criteria when assessing submissions: </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1wtKAjpvEiMWn-RpFDi_2Vqcvt5i3sCFPmUt3MtsKOjo/edit?tab=t.v8o9nnadfvtm"><span>Epistemic Case Study Competition - Judging Criteria</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>In addition to the potential prizes, strong entries that demonstrate real promise may also lead to an offer for further funded work with us (we estimate an 75% chance that a $50k-winning entry receives an offer like this).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefovmn7jlwa4n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnovmn7jlwa4n">[6]</a></sup></span></p><h3><b><span>Please use </span></b><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLScHGLJRH5ex27i0hpL0wPqyZFqp1ykYqFmJxgvg_zYKD6g1mw/viewform"><b><span>this linked form</span></b></a><b><span> to submit your entry; entries are due by Jul 19, 2026.</span></b></h3><p><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1rTX-Z23HuR68E9CKn87FmrMin9w_rOFjStr3HgzwlXA/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.9kv9jflko3ab"><span>FLF’s general contest rules</span></a><span> apply.</span></p><h2><b><span>Prize structure</span></b></h2><p><span>We’ve allocated roughly $200k for this competition with the size of any individual award reflecting how much an entry moves us. We'd rather award fewer, larger prizes for entries that genuinely impress us than spread the pool out. If a wave of strong work arrives, we'll happily expand the total prize pool.</span></p><p><span>Concretely, we expect to award up to:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>$50k</span></b><span> → for an entry or entries we find truly inspiring. The kind of submission that changes how we think about the problem or that we'd want to point to as a new reference point for AI-assisted epistemic work. We may not award it at all – or – we may also award it more than once if multiple entries clear that bar.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>$5k to $50k</span></b><span> → for entries that meaningfully advance the state of the art, whether across the full stack or on a well-defined piece of it (ingestion, structure, or assessment). The size of each award reflects how far the work pushes the field and we expect several entries to land somewhere in this range.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Continuation funding</span></b><span> → beyond prize money, we expect to fund individuals or teams to keep building, on terms agreed case by case. For the strongest entries this may be the real prize: an ongoing relationship with FLF and a path to sustained work on the stack. We'll raise this with finalists after judging.</span></li></ul><h2><b><span>Interested in participating or following along?</span></b></h2><p><span>Want to compete, follow along, or join the conversation? </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSeBqNCI4Klaq6FO8CbhYCxr6cYAUMjeosExOjatfCHYfEvNVQ/viewform?usp=header"><b><span>Express interest</span></b></a><span> to receive updates, commentary, and see how you can participate as the competition unfolds.</span></p><h2><b><span>Why we're doing this</span></b></h2><p><span>We're building toward what we call a</span><a href="https://flf.org/projects/epistack/"><span> </span></a><a href="https://flf.org/projects/epistack/"><span>full epistemic stack</span></a><span>, layered infrastructure for making the provenance, structure, and assessment of knowledge transparent and traversable at scale. We think recent AI advances make this newly tractable, but the hard problems are in methodology and workflow design, as well as usability, not just capability.</span></p><p><span>Not only do we expect these tools to be of widespread benefit, but we expect some organizations like ours to be </span><a href="https://flf.org/timelines/"><span>eager early adopters</span></a><span>. FLF hopes to meaningfully inform its strategy and prioritisation based on insights from these tools, meaning that great work here could move millions of dollars per year and help us (and others) be more effective.</span></p><h2><span>We're excited to see what you build.</span></h2><p><i><span>Much gratitude to Ben Goldhaber (formerly FLF), Joel Chan, Saif Haobsh, Austin Chen, Andreas Stuhlmüller, and Dustin Kimmel for contributions and feedback.</span></i></p><h2><b><span>The case studies</span></b></h2><h3><span>COVID</span></h3><p><span>In early 2024, a $100,000 judged debate took place between Saar Wilf (founder of Rootclaim) and Peter Miller on the origins of COVID-19. Over 15 hours of structured argument, two smart people marshalled epidemiological data, viral genetics, Bayesian inference, and institutional analysis to reach opposite conclusions. Two expert judges ruled decisively for zoonosis. Six independent Bayesian analyses of the same evidence spanned 23 orders of magnitude.</span></p><p><span>For more read Scott Alexander’s</span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/practically-a-book-review-rootclaim"><span> </span></a><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/practically-a-book-review-rootclaim"><span>detailed writeup</span></a><span>. We feel that the debate videos, judge decisions, and comment threads it links to form one of the richest publicly available records of a complex real-world epistemic dispute on an important issue.</span></p><p><span>And yet all this information is still incredibly difficult to navigate, interrogate, and use to inform one’s beliefs.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>It requires significant background expertise to understand the state of play of the debate and make a considered judgement. The debate was overseen by two judges with PhDs who work as a professional microbiologist and applied mathematician, respectively.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The format, a live video debate, may not be the optimal way for a judge to interact with the material.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefd68aj3nrhcl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnd68aj3nrhcl">[7]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span>Further, this intense epistemic effort represents a point in time in a conversation which continues to evolve.</span></p><p><span>We feel this makes it a strong stress test for tools and methods that aim to make reasoning more transparent, traversable, updateable, and trustworthy.</span></p><p><span>Your job: craft the AI-assisted methodologies that build a structure to help people navigate this topic successfully.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0cpt6c678hh5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0cpt6c678hh5">[8]</a></sup></span></p><h4><span>Starting material</span></h4><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/practically-a-book-review-rootclaim"><span>Scott Alexander's writeup of the COVID origins debate</span></a><span> (the core case material)</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://drive.google.com/file/d/1YhmkYB32RpGsXvQTsX4xZ0Yul1wiwh8Z/view"><span>Judge Will's decision</span></a><span> | </span><a href="https://drive.google.com/file/d/1aHlhPd-16EOabzXhiajT5PBm3uVCAG3T/view"><span>Judge Eric's decision</span></a></li><li value="3"><a href="https://michaelweissman.substack.com/p/an-inconvenient-probability-v57"><span>Michael Weissman's Bayesian analysis</span></a><span> (an example of a more rigorous independent analysis)</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://blog.rootclaim.com/covid-origins-debate-response-to-scott-alexander/"><span>Rootclaim's response</span></a></li><li value="5"><span>The debate videos: </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y1vaooTKHCM"><span>Session 1</span></a><span> | </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KdORmvU8MLI"><span>Session 2</span></a><span> | </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=d1dbfoK8nSE"><span>Session 3</span></a></li></ul><h3><span>Black holes</span></h3><p><span>CERN, home of the world’s largest particle accelerator, the Large Hadron Collider (LHC), has a frequently asked question: </span><a href="https://home.cern/resources/faqs/will-cern-generate-black-hole"><span>Will CERN generate a black hole?</span></a></p><p><span>What??</span></p><p><span>As in </span><a href="https://blog.nuclearsecrecy.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/06/1946-LA-602-Konopinski-Marvin-Teller-Ignition-fo-the-Atmsophere.pdf"><span>some previous science experiments</span></a><span>, noting that novel circumstances might produce unprecedented outcomes, some participants had apocalyptic concerns. How were these put to rest? (Were they truly? What does that hinge on?)</span></p><p><span>Unlike COVID, this is (we hope!) essentially a closed case, and uncontested. It nevertheless rests on a huge body of accumulated and interacting knowledge which enabled scientists (and the officials and public supporting them) to move forward with confidence.</span></p><p><span>The key challenge here may be in probing this argument for its dependencies and key considerations, and perhaps noting the weakest or most speculative points — all in an accessible way.</span></p><h3><span>Eggs</span></h3><p><span>Are eggs good to eat? Bad to eat? Great in moderation? How can we tell? Does it vary across people, and what predicts this? What else should we be paying attention to here?</span></p><p><span>This vague and open-ended topic, though mundane, is representative of a huge number of everyday questions — and hopefully also a microcosm of many more impactful debates. Sometimes getting resolution on </span><i><span>what are the important things to answer</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>what are the appropriate ways of knowing</span></i><span> is (more than) half of the challenge.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnixb7bh40l7e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefixb7bh40l7e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Forecasting the shape and capability of future AI is difficult, but we are excited to imagine a world where epistemic investigations of this (and greater!) quality are commonplace. We’re aiming to catalyse that path through activities like this competition.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz7clpnyrzud"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz7clpnyrzud">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>By </span><i><span>structure</span></i><span>, we mean capturing the relations between different sources, claims, authors, and so on. Who said what and when? What evidence or reasons support that? What counterarguments exist or reasons for doubt? We give further ideas below. Keeping this structure alive means less loss by compression, and preserving </span><a href="https://aiprospects.substack.com/p/when-ideas-round-to-false"><span>space for nuance</span></a><span> — even if we don’t consume it right away.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4u73i3qa14"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4u73i3qa14">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The human urge to apply technology to knowledge-provision isn’t new: consider libraries, citations, indexes, encyclopedias (including Wikipedia), databases, web search — all of which push the frontier in this space.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrxxyn1anfzf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrxxyn1anfzf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Written discussions should aim to not exceed 10 pages, not including appendix-like material and worked examples. Worked examples and fully-fledged example knowledge bases can be arbitrarily sized (within reason) but should be navigable. Consider including curated pointers to particularly effective regions of worked examples. Code should either be brief, legible (pseudo)code or well-documented and ready to install and run with close to a single click. </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1wtKAjpvEiMWn-RpFDi_2Vqcvt5i3sCFPmUt3MtsKOjo/edit?tab=t.s5p8ga2p1drq"><span>See here</span></a><span> for more detail.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn95g2ovdewj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref95g2ovdewj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Ideally such that judges can easily reimplement on a new case.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnovmn7jlwa4n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefovmn7jlwa4n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>One type of further work might be incorporating workflows into forecasting and prediction — perhaps grounded in forecasting bot competitions.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnd68aj3nrhcl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefd68aj3nrhcl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Rootclaim thinks that one reason they lost the debate was that the “structure provided a major advantage to the debater with more memorized knowledge of the issue”.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0cpt6c678hh5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0cpt6c678hh5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We envision these as acting as eventually becoming living knowledge bases, not merely snapshots in time.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/frizRHnA6AZpJSDqw/lab-leaks-black-holes-and-eggs-epistemic-case-study#comments">Discuss</a>

### [AI #171: False Flag](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LzxoR5GakceQFtbta/ai-171-false-flag)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>This was the week of Claude Opus 4.8. <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/claude-opus-48-is-honestly-better?r=67wny"><strong>I covered the model card</strong></a>, then <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/opus-48-part-2-model-welfare?r=67wny"><strong>model welfare</strong></a> concerns, and finally <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/claude-opus-48-capabilities-and-reactions?r=67wny"><strong>capabilities and reactions</strong></a>. It’s a good model, sir, an incremental but real improvement over Opus 4.7, and it is now my clear daily driver. <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/trump-signs-executive-order-for-ai?r=67wny"><strong>The Trump Executive Order returned from being seemingly dead</strong></a>, officially putting us in the prior restraint era of frontier model releases, even if they do not call it that. There are some worrisome details, especially around putting too much responsibility on the NSA rather than CAISI and classifying the testing process, and things could go in very bad directions, but I am tentatively happy about this on net.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25323"></span>


</div>
<p>OpenAI <a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/25752ecb-0e5c-47f9-b9e4-c0f4d76f8d3d/a-blueprint-for-a-federal-framework.pdf"><strong>offered us a new policy blueprint</strong></a>. It seems remarkably good, and I want to hold off on my full coverage to give it the attention it deserves, likely in its own post. By contrast, their political operations are also engaged in some rather terrible activities, which I do cover here.</p>

<h4>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/05/doc-in-a-box.html?utm_source=rss&amp;utm_medium=rss&amp;utm_campaign=doc-in-a-box">Doc In a Box</a> <a href="https://commerce.utah.gov/wp-content/uploads/2026/05/Doctronic-Outcomes-May-2026.pdf">is performing well so far in Utah</a>. They are focusing on avoiding false positives, at the risk of false negatives, since without AI it’s all negatives, and escalation is a small mistake.</p>
<blockquote><p>In the 72% of cases where the AI recommend a refill at least one of two physicians agreed in 97% of cases. In the 28% of Cases Where the AI Escalated to a Physician Without Recommending Renewal <img alt="▪" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LzxoR5GakceQFtbta/b71bleqnkss6xv0xmhdx" style="height: 1em;" /> When the AI declined to recommend renewal without further information, a human telehealth appointment was arranged. <img alt="▪" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LzxoR5GakceQFtbta/b71bleqnkss6xv0xmhdx" style="height: 1em;" /> For these patients, 69% of physician reviews agreed that the escalation was appropriate, and more information was needed to authorize a renewal. <img alt="▪" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LzxoR5GakceQFtbta/b71bleqnkss6xv0xmhdx" style="height: 1em;" /> In the other 31% of cases, the physician determined the escalation was overly cautious. <img alt="▪" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LzxoR5GakceQFtbta/b71bleqnkss6xv0xmhdx" style="height: 1em;" /> For a new system like this, overcaution is appropriate and welcome. In the long term, reducing overcaution without compromising safety would improve patient access to care, but we aren’t rushing to see that happen.</p></blockquote>
<p>A 97% rate of refills being at least reasonable seems very good. I doubt physicians agree with each other more often than that. Having only about 50% more escalations than were necessary also seems very strong. Big success here, unless the false positives are unusually dangerous for some reason, but we see no sign of ths. Using only a graph with numerical values, <a href="https://x.com/cremieuxrecueil/status/2060095960064106812">track down the original paper in order to get a higher resolution version</a>. <a href="https://x.com/ben_r_hoffman/status/2060158200003883129">Asking a blank-slate AI is a good way to tap into</a> ‘general common sense’ intuitions. <a href="https://www.bain.com/insights/synthetic-customers-earn-their-stripes/">Use synthetic customers to accelerate product development and test marketing</a>. They are not perfect, and you want to augment rather than replace talking to and testing with real customers, but the synthetic ones can already be remarkably good. It is certainly a good first test for new ideas or features. The next logical step, which may or may not be a terrible idea, is to generate and iterate on synthetic ideas in bulk using synthetic customers. <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/05/28/technology/sell-house-with-ai-no-realtor.html">Sell your house.</a> Stuart Thompson lets Gemini (because he had a free account there from work that saved him $8 a month?!) walk him through everything involved in the sale, including being his agent. The problem is, Stuart does not seem to realize he does not know the counterfactual?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://archive.is/K9K2F#selection-1241.0-1243.95">Stuart A. Thompson</a>: <strong>In the end, using A.I. netted me more than $90,000. </strong>That includes the premium over the asking price, plus the roughly $36,000 in fees I didn’t pay.</p></blockquote>
<p>I mean, yes, the agents he talked to early on told him he’d lose money, and instead he turned a profit. But only after the sale did he talk to another agent for an expert opinion, and that expert expected a higher sale price than Stuart got, meaning he almost certainly listed too low. Stuart thinks that after the agent fee he still basically broke even, but I’m guessing he put in more work and stress this way, and took on more downside risk. I know that if I am ever selling or buying, I will be using AI extensively as part of the effort, but I am going to stick with <a href="https://www.sothebysrealty.com/eng/associate/180-a-1178-40027037/danielle-wiedemann">Danielle Wiedemann</a>. I am confident that her help, connections and advice were worth far more than the fee, and would be again. <a href="https://x.com/giansegato/status/2061285376548077614">Save your presentation</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/giansegato/status/2061285376548077614">gian</a>: spent my 11-hour flight back from europe working on a very long report. started as a <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/slack">slack</a> message but morphed into a several pages long doc. wifi was as shitty as it gets. after finally making it home i realized that the computer had forcefully restarted. opened <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/slack">slack</a>: draft was gone :( hail mary: claude pls save me, no clue how but pls try it checked APFS snapshots, time machine, <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/slack">slack</a> indexeddb, write-ahead logs, service worker / http caches, local storage, app logs, hibernation image… nothing. all gone but then… it realized i have alfred installed. so it checked the clipboard snapshots alfred keeps in sqlite. sad news: alfred clipboard memory gets deleted after 24h. aggressive retention policy. however! when sqlite runs DELETE, nothing gets actually deleted. it only marks pages as reusable, but it doesn’t override the physical bytes. so claude decided to do a raw-scan of the db, reverse eng alfred data format, figure out the portion containing the timestamp, stitched everything back together across overflow pages… and handed me the exact final version of my report, the last one i cmd+C’d all this, in a single shot … day 200 of “what if you had an elite hacker you can ask anything to”</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes, it was user error to get into this spot in the first place. Still counts. <a href="https://claude.com/blog/how-anthropic-enables-self-service-data-analytics-with-claude">Anthropic guide to how Anthropic ‘enables self-service data analytics with Claude</a>.’</p>


<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/alexalbert__/status/2060075667342151767">Reminder that Claude’s ‘adaptive thinking’ setting means ‘thinking</a>’ so if you turn it off you are turning off thinking. Very bad UI, but leave it on.</p>


<h4>Huh, Upgrades</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2060428604727771421">Codex computer use, and ability to be controlled from a phone, expands to Windows</a>. <a href="https://openai.com/index/codex-for-every-role-tool-workflow/">Codex adds role-specific plugins, sites and annotations</a>. Early plugins include: Data analytics, creative production, sales, product design, public equity investing and investment banking. More are coming soon. <a href="https://openai.com/index/openai-frontier-models-and-codex-are-now-available-on-aws/">OpenAI Codex and models now available on Amazon Bedrock</a>. <a href="https://x.com/michpokrass/status/2060219759682330970">There is a new</a> <a href="https://help.openai.com/en/articles/6825453-chatgpt-release-notes">version of GPT-5.5-Instant</a>. I’m glad we’re doing a lot less of this silent updating, if you want to move to GPT-5.5.1-Instant then by all means do so. <a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2061947411141169494">Claude Code changes the clone session command from /fork to /branch</a>, with the new /fork meaning ‘spin up a background agent to help.’ Claude Code realizes its mistake, <a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2062257177788858398">changes the dynamic workflow trigger word from ‘workflow’ to ‘ultracode.</a>’ <a href="https://x.com/joshwoodward/status/2062025667852812583">Gemini finally lets you adjust thinking levels across Web, iOS and Android</a>, although this is kind of odd when Gemini 3.5 Flash is the best they can do. <a href="https://x.com/googlegemma/status/2062202706882883696">Gemma-4-12B now exists</a> and can run locally with 16GB of memory.</p>


<h4>On Your Marks</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/MParakhin/status/2062200836021969314">Opus 4.8 takes the top spot in Toloka Aren</a>a. Mikhail Parakhin calls it a big step forward, says the base model and instruction following are still inferior to GPT-5.5, but they use more tokens and it’s better at coding, math and reasoning.</p>


<h4>Choose Your Fighter</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/quxiaoyin/status/2059666365280133623">DeepSeek v4 is fast and permanently very cheap</a>, remarkably close to free. Sure. But the marginal value of a better job is an absolute measure, not a relative one. In general I continue to recommend paying up for quality unless you’re serving to others at scale.</p>


<h4>Get My Agent On The Line</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.salesforce.com/news/stories/how-engineering-became-agentic/">Salesforce report on the agentic shift within their engineering department</a>, which standardized around Claude Code with no token limits. <a href="https://x.com/adocomplete/status/2062019442692006170">You can use /goal with Claude Code overnight</a>, but you can also be interrupted. It seems like we should have ways to automatically resume on interruption or push through one, soon, especially if it’s something like ‘laptop decides to update’?</p>


<h4>Cyber Lack of Security</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/expanding-project-glasswing">Project Glasswing expands</a> to an additional ~150 organizations, for a total of ~200, based on more than 15 countries, including giving access to the EU. They are also releasing some of their tools. <a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2060321428596027499">Apple can be remarkably stingy with its bug bounties</a>. That’s not going to cut it. <a href="https://x.com/ZenoEleatis/status/2061383880670662810">Microsoft also seems to not be treating independent security researchers</a> so well? <a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2061457252008051105">Palo Alto Networks is finding five times as many critical vulnerabilities</a> as it did before Mythos, at the cost of a $1 million Mythos token bill in several weeks. This is framed as a lot, but their overall R&amp;D budget is $1.3 to $1.6 billion per year, probably with ~$135m-$250m in annual costs for Unit 42. So this seems both highly affordable and way more efficient than their previous strategies. But yes, more work to do, now. <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/AI-enabled-cyber-threats-mitre-attack">Anthropic analyzed 832 accounts that got banned for cyberattacks</a> in the past year. They find that the percentage posing medium or higher treat level jumped from 33% to 56% from the first to second half of the year, and AI use rose. <a href="https://x.com/JeffLadish/status/2061957709747155375">On a personal level, be sure to protect yourself with at least the basic things</a>, to stay ahead of the broad based hacking attempts that will only increase with time, for anything you care about protecting. You absolutely should not think ‘oh I am already hacked’ because hacks can be very disruptive or costly, and most attempts are low effort and defense in depth, or even defense in minimal depth, goes a long way.</p>


<h4>Deepfaketown and Botpocalypse Soon</h4>


<p><a href="https://news.uchicago.edu/story/song-ai-generated-uchicago-scientists-create-browser-extension-check?utm_source=uc_twitter&amp;utm_medium=social&amp;utm_content=news">Almost half of new songs uploaded to online music platforms like Spotify are now AI</a>. We can know this because there are subtle artifacts that can get picked up by tools like Quicksilver, even if humans can’t hear the difference. Of course, 50% of uploads is very different from 50% of plays. Almost all music gets almost no listens. What is real? How do you define real? Do you actually care about this… real?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2060359754380202369">Joe Weisenthal</a>: I said this to @citrini last night, but in the future, will we really need storage? I take a ton of photos of my kids, and they are on my phone and in a cloud. But in the future, won’t I just tell a model “generate a photo from my son’s 7th birthday” and it’ll be just as good? <a href="https://x.com/citrini/status/2060367867795611959">Citrini</a>: I thought my Chinese AI hardware take was spicy and then you hit me with the “do we really need authentic memories of our loved ones when the matrix can freely provide them?” <a href="https://x.com/Romaniiitedomum/status/2060359980843257975">Augsburg Traders</a>: Don’t really need a son either if we’re being dark about it. <a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2060360109373509977">Joe Weisenthal</a>: Yeah li’l dark. But yeah, society’s going to get weird. <a href="https://x.com/ASandhu/status/2060777630593638633">Amandeep Sandhu <img alt="✌" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cQizFzEvZ8esKJGST/lp1nogd7awyssnnlbxx3" style="height: 1em;" />︎ ㋡</a>: Seems like you are trying to get out of hosting a birthday party for your 7 year old <a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2060777845375242244">Joe Weisenthal</a>: Well I’m hosting in 20 minutes, so too late to get out of that one</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes, we care about this real, even if the quality of the photo becomes indistinguishable or ‘just as good.’ I hope.</p>


<h4>You Didn’t Write That</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/pangramlabs/status/2059998138073542778">A majority of Doctor of Education dissertations contain AI-generated text</a>, although it is usually a minority of text. Text more than a few years old reliably scores exactly 0%.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LzxoR5GakceQFtbta/rbikqrmnpin2eyl7xlz4" /></figure>


<div>&nbsp;</div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2060063390161358945">Kelsey Piper takes a stab at why this is bad</a>, but fails to consider that the context is an Education dissertation, where the less work you put into it the better off we all are. <a href="https://x.com/zdch/status/2060082469525713265">Zac Hill points out he does get false positives</a> (in the 15% range) on Pangram, but this is because he is copying text from outside. So that’s not really a full false positive, but it does mean that a percentage like that doesn’t have to involve direct AI use. Pangram is easy to fool if you put in some effort. Here is another example.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/IsaacKing314/status/2059932473941954623">Isaac King</a>: I asked Claude to write an essay that fools Pangram, so it wrote an essay, then wrote a script to convert most of the ASCII letters to Cyrillic lookalike characters. It worked. <a href="https://x.com/PersonaIData/status/2060002871949644070">Kingston Jr</a>: I asked Claude to do the same and it started scolding me</p></blockquote>
<p>That will fool Pangram, but leaves a rather conspicuous artifact. This is a place where defense-in-depth is valuable. You need to fool the AI detector without looking to a human like you are trying to fool the AI detector. <a href="https://x.com/boazbaraktcs/status/2060838437352022286">You can also fool Pangram the other way, even at 13, if you try hard enough</a>. And sure, obviously any fixed target is going to have issues when people actively try to fool it. The point is that most AI efforts are attempts to save effort, and thus are not trying so hard, and humans presumably want to look human. There is the question of how to present the results.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/EigenGender/status/2062317571224048039">EigenGender is going to vibecamp</a>: I gotta say I don’t have first hand evidence about how reliable pangram is but the fact that they give a percentage score that doesn’t have any obviously sane interpretation doesn’t make me feel great <a href="https://x.com/sethlazar/status/2062331065856049387">Seth Lazar</a>: This is something I’d encourage @max_spero_ to change actually. I think pangram is v reliable (for now; this is an adversarial and dynamic setting) but that its way of representing results risks misleading. What it actually means is that “75% of the characters (possibly tokens, possibly words) falls in a 250-400 word window which the model has give at least a 0.75 probability of containing AI”. That’s not the easiest result to communicate I understand, but still there should be something one can click on that says it. We’ve done extensive experiments (though we could certainly do more) and it really doesn’t trigger for normal copy-editing. That said, we did narrow the window down to 100w (<a href="https://blog.neurips.cc/2026/06/02/ai-generated-papers-in-the-neurips-2026-position-paper-track/">below that you get a lot more false negatives</a>). <a href="https://x.com/EigenGender/status/2062527943256190996">EigenGender</a>: I think that people’s lives are being changed by the results of the software and it’s on pangram to properly communicate uncertainty Seth Lazar: Pangram is incredibly good at identifying AI-generated text. The FPR is minuscule. After that, I think it’s more a matter of figuring out what the norms actually should be.</p></blockquote>
<p>We do want to find the right way to communicate the results and maximize it, but I think what Pangram is doing now is fine and I don’t see an obviously better option, and Man in the Arena and avoid <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/QXpxioWSQcNuNnNTy/the-copenhagen-interpretation-of-ethics">the Copenhagen Interpretation of Ethics</a> apply. AIs struggle even with simple things, like <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2060907387888820304">rhythm and using enough verbs</a>, because they’re maximizing locally. As Mark Twain might put it, even when they know the words, they lack the music.</p>


<h4>Copyright Confrontation</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/venturetwins/status/2060472537847562523">Hollywood is starting to get over their original negative reactions and use AI tools for production</a>, as the economics and creative opportunities are too good, even now. This will only accelerate from here.</p>


<h4>They Took Our Jobs</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2061074519016477158">For those confused about the radiology example, yes</a>, AI is better than radiologists at reading x-rays, and many other components of professional services, and does so at cost epsilon, and this is super useful. Even if no one is out of work quite yet, often there is a ton of value in ‘pretty good answer, vastly better than you could otherwise get without a professional, for cost ~$0’ when the professional costs $1,000 and up. <a href="https://x.com/garrytan/status/2061076595474121073">Garry Tan thinks ‘the models are smart enough</a>’ to replace 90% of your employees.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2060826905910141360">Paul Graham</a>: This problem will naturally tend to go away as companies are grown from the start using AI. Then you don’t need to extract any domain knowledge from people’s heads; it will never have been in people’s heads.</p></blockquote>
<p>So the ‘problem’ is that you needed 90% of your employees, at all, in the first place? <a href="https://newsletter.semianalysis.com/p/ai-dark-output-the-visible-cost-of">Much AI productivity takes and will take the form of ‘dark output,’</a> meaning it does not show up in the traditional statistics like GDP. We can price the tokens, the cost paid, but their marginal productivity goes untracked, either entirely or it gets subsumed elsewhere. This is a measurement issue, not a real issue, so we should not be surprised to see GDP and productivity rise remarkably fast while people say ‘only [X]%’ of it can ‘be attributed’ to AI. Uber CEO Dara reports that they blew out their whole ‘AI budget’ for the year in a single quarter. This made their engineers ‘superhuman’ in their productivity, and he’s pushing for more adoption, but he’s ‘metering’ headcount. His claim is ‘he’s using the premium models to explore’ and then he’ll ‘bring in more efficient models.’ Who wants to tell him? The new solution seems to be <a href="https://x.com/negligible_cap/status/2061855862637973750">a $1,500 monthly cap on AI coding tools for staff</a>. Uber stock moved up that day, which (if it is responding to this) is a wrong way move.</p>


<h4>They Taxed Our Jobs</h4>


<p><a href="https://writing.antonleicht.me/cp/200603941">Anton Leicht and Dean Ball team up</a> to write about what we should do about potential job loss due to AI, from the perspective of prospective ‘de facto normal technology’ AI worlds even if they don’t call it that. They wisely say we don’t know what will happen, and that the ‘no regrets’ actions will be insufficient so solve the problem, but expect the world to stay normal enough, and humans competitive and useful enough, that we can use traditional solutions to such problems. They start with easy wins.</p>
<ol>
<li>Even footing: Equalize tax treatment of AI versus labor. Yes, please.</li>
<li>Retraining: Bolster workforce training and development. They notice they are skeptical in practice, and I am even more skeptical, but sure, we can try it.</li>
<li>Measurement: Know what is happening. Yes, of course.</li>
</ol>
<p>Then they recommend what they call difficult bets.</p>
<ol>
<li>Junior Job Subsidy.</li>
</ol>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://writing.antonleicht.me/cp/200603941">Anton Leicht</a> and Dean Ball: We put to you that <strong>the solution to deal with junior job losses might be to keep these jobs around by brute force</strong> for a while, so that the critically important economic incentive to explore <em>how</em> to use junior workers does not cease. More specifically, we might do so by restructuring the tax code to subsidize junior employment.</p></blockquote>
<p>Given who is saying to keep jobs around by brute force, by which they mean tax incentives, we should listen. This seems like a good use of progressive taxation, which we want to do anyway, to stack the deck in favor of hiring more young workers and those switching industries, presumably with phase outs for high earners. This risks distortions if taken too far (e.g. dumping senior workers for subsidized junior workers, or gaming designations), the marginal value of young workers could easily fall below zero marginal product if there is no future for them, and gating to particular industries or occupations risks going into ‘picking winners and losers’ and other similar dangerous territories and opportunities for corruption and pork. The authors are well aware, and are pushing anyway. The main solution they offer is, again, taxes. They suggest doing so via raising corporate taxes, despite this having a long track record of being highly economically damaging. You definitely need to avoid worse distortions, and you definitely do not want a ‘token tax’ as such for this reason, although a tax on compute is non-crazy. Taking a stake in frontier developers is definitely an error. They quickly dismiss consumption taxes as having a fatal perception problem, despite them being objectively the efficient answer, because they raise prices and signaling is too important here. I found this disappointing, and there are ways to fix this and also make the tax progressive. It would be great if humans remained fundamentally highly productive while we collectively got far wealthier due to AI, so all we needed to do was redistribution and moving the tax code around. Alas, no, I do not expect we live in such a convenient world. At which point, we likely have bigger problems, but also employment does not get solved with basic tax code shifts. If we stay in control somehow then we could do progressive redistribution to keep food on the table and a roof over people’s heads, but the jobs will vanish, or they will be rather fully fake.</p>


<h4>The Art of the Jailbreak</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2061522982107791633">Who needs a jailbreak</a>? You can just take anyone’s Instagram account at will, or rather you could for a brief period. <a href="https://thecybersecguru.com/news/instagram-meta-ai-vulnerability-account-recovery-exploit/">A full report is here</a>. No, they haven’t (as of Monday, anyway) reverted the bulk of the impacted accounts yet.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LzxoR5GakceQFtbta/vvrecetp1izaga0mv7h9" /></figure>


<div>&nbsp;</div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/oracles/status/2061331778972114948">André</a>: Today Instagram had this massive exploit where hackers were just stealing rare handles left and right. Hundreds of accounts gone. People losing handles they’ve owned since 2010, some worth hundreds of thousands. I own a few rare ones so I was actually stressed watching this happen in real time, which I haven’t been in years. Obama White House account got hit. These aren’t some random new accounts, these are verified, locked down accounts and they still got compromised. The thing is the exploit is so simple it’s almost funny. Attacker goes to Forgot Password, says their account is hacked, turns on a VPN to match the target’s location (which now you can find on the about section of the page). Instagram’s AI support flow asks them to verify with a selfie. They grab a photo from the target’s profile, run it through an AI video generator to make an animation of the person’s face moving around, upload that to Meta’s AI as proof. And Meta’s AI just accepts it because it can’t tell the difference between a real selfie and an AI-generated video of someone’s face. Once verified they change the email to theirs. Password reset link goes to their email. They own it now. 2FA gets bypassed somehow in the process but honestly I don’t know exactly how, just that it did. Point is even locked down accounts went down. Then you try to recover your account and you’re talking to a chatbot that has zero ability to help. You can’t escalate to a human. You’re just stuck. Your asset is gone and there’s no one to call. Meta took hours to even acknowledge it while accounts were getting stolen every minute. Now thankfully it’s patched but I don’t think it will be the last one. Stay safe!</p></blockquote>
<p>I cleverly protected my own Instagram handle by having no posts, which meant there was no selfie there to steal.</p>

Mentors come from Anthropic, DeepMind, OpenAI, ARC, and more.
